A well-posedness result for the compressible two-fluid model with density-dependent viscosity
Sagbo Marcel ZODJI

Abstract
. In this paper, we study a system of PDEs describing the motion of two compressible viscous fluids occupying the whole space ℝ d ​ ( d ∈ { 2 , 3 } CLOSE \mathbb{R}^{d}\;(d\in\{2,3\} ). The two phases of the mixture are separated by a 𝒞 1 + α \mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha} -regular sharp interface 𝒞 \mathcal{C} across which the density can experience jumps. We prove the existence of a unique local-in-time solution assuming that the initial density is α \alpha -Hölder continuous on both sides of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . The initial velocity belongs to the Sobolev space H 1 ​ ( ℝ d ) H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , and the divergence of the initial stress tensor belongs to L 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . The later assumption expresses somehow the continuity of the stress tensor. This result is more general than the one by Tani [ 32 ] , as it allows for less regular initial data and furthermore it can serve as a building block for the construction of global-in-time solutions.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
76N10, 35A02, 76T10, 35Q30

1.
Introduction and main result

1.1.
Presentation of the model

In this paper, we are concerned with the following system of PDE describing the evolution of two compressible fluids with density-dependent viscosity in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\;(d\in\{2,3\})
,

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho_{\pm}+\dvg(\rho_{\pm}u)=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho u^{j})+\dvg(\rho u^{j}u)=\dvg\Pi^{j}.\end{cases}

Above,
\rho_{\pm}=\rho_{\pm}(t,x)\geqslant 0
is the density of the phase fluid labelled
\pm
,
\rho:=\rho_{+}+\rho_{-}
is the total
density of the mixture and
u=u(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the single velocity of the mixture. The stress tensor
\Pi
is given by

(1.2)

\displaystyle\Pi^{jk}=2\mu\mathbb{D}^{jk}u+(\lambda\dvg u-P+\widetilde{P})\delta^{jk}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbb{D}^{jk}u=\dfrac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{j}u^{k}+\partial_{k}u^{j}\big),

where
P=P(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
,
\mu=\mu(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
,
\lambda=\lambda(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
are respectively pressure, dynamic and kinematic viscosity of the mixture and are given
\mathscr{C}^{2}
-regular functions of
\rho_{\pm}
, and finally
\widetilde{P}
is a positive equilibrium pressure. We refer to
[
16
]
for the derivation of (
1.1
) for the more general case of heat conducting fluids mixture.

It is assumed that the two pure components of the mixture are immiscible, so that each fluid is implicitly assumed to occupy its own domain and that
\rho_{+}\rho_{-}=0
. In particular they are separated by a sharp interface
\mathcal{C}
. The relevant physical situation for multi-phase flows is exactly where the total density
\rho
experiences discontinuity across the interface
\mathcal{C}
. Since the two densities
\rho_{\pm}
of the mixture are assumed to be different, we see that the class of
piecewise regular
functions is suitable for the study of this system. This motivates the following definitions.

1.1.1.
Piecewise regularity

(1)

Throughout this paper
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and we assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a compact
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular manifold of dimension
d-1
, which is the boundary of an open, bounded and simply connected domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It is defined as follows.

There exists a finite number
J\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
of open bounded sets
V_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
,
j\in\llbracket 1,J\rrbracket
and maps

\gamma_{j}\colon V_{j}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\mathcal{C}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{J}\gamma_{j}(V_{j}),

such that
\gamma_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}(V_{j})
are homeomorphisms on their images. For
(j,k)\in\llbracket 1,J\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,J\rrbracket
such that
\gamma_{j}(V_{j})\cap\gamma_{k}(V_{k})\neq\emptyset
, the map

\gamma_{j}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{k}\colon\gamma_{k}^{-1}\big(\gamma_{j}(V_{j})\cap\gamma_{k}(V_{k})\big)\longrightarrow\gamma_{j}^{-1}\big(\gamma_{j}(V_{j})\cap\gamma_{k}(V_{k})\big)

is a
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism. Furthermore, these mappings provide an analytical expression for the outward normal vector field
n_{x}
on
\mathcal{C}
. We introduce the following notations:

\left\lVert\mathcal{C}\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}:=\sup_{j}\lVert\nabla\gamma_{j}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(V_{j})},\,\text{ and }\,\left\lVert\mathcal{C}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}:=\sup_{j}\lVert\nabla\gamma_{j}\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(V_{j})}.

Following
[
11
, page 6]
, we define the quantity:

(1.3)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{C}\right\rvert_{*}:=\Bigg(\inf_{j}\bigg(\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\neq s^{\prime}\\
s,s^{\prime}\in V_{j}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\lvert\gamma_{j}(s)-\gamma_{j}(s^{\prime})\rvert}{\lvert s-s^{\prime}\rvert}\bigg)\Bigg)^{-1}\in(0,\infty).

For
(j,k)\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
, we define the second-order
Riesz operator as
\mathcal{R}_{jk}=-\partial_{j}(-\Delta)^{-1}\partial_{k}
. The piecewise Hölder estimate in
A.1
for the operator
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{R}_{jk}
(respectively,
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{R}_{jk}\mathcal{R}_{lm}
) involves a polynomial denoted by
\mathfrak{P}^{jk}
(respectively,
\mathfrak{P}^{jklm}
). Next, we consider a polynomial
\mathfrak{P}
that satisfies:

\mathfrak{P}^{jk}(a),\,\mathfrak{P}^{jklm}(a)\leqslant\mathfrak{P}(a),\;\text{ for all }\;a>0.

Finally, we introduce the following quantity:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}}:=\big(1+\left\lvert\mathcal{C}\right\rvert\big)\mathfrak{P}\big(\left\lVert\mathcal{C}\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}+\left\lvert\mathcal{C}\right\rvert_{*}\big)\left\lVert\mathcal{C}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}.

As noted in
[
20
, Section 3.1]
(see also
[
10
, Remark 2, page 35]
), there exists a function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
such that

D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\varphi(x)>0\},\quad\text{and}\quad\lvert\nabla\varphi\rvert_{\text{inf}}:=\inf_{x\in\partial D}\lvert\nabla\varphi(x)\rvert>0.

We then define:

(1.5)

\displaystyle\ell_{\varphi}=\min\left\{1,\left(\dfrac{\lvert\nabla\varphi\rvert_{\text{inf}}}{\lVert\nabla\varphi\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}}}\right)^{1/\alpha}\right\}.

(2)

Since

\mathbb{R}^{d}=D\cup\mathcal{C}\cup(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{D}),

we define the space of piecewise
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions with respect to
\mathcal{C}
as follows:

\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{D})\cap\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus D),\;\,\text{with}\;\,\lVert g\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\lVert g\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{D})}+\lVert g\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus D)}

and the non-homogeneous space

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;\text{ with }\;\lVert g\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert g\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This space strictly contains the Hölder space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(3)

Given a function
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the jump
\llbracket g\rrbracket
, and the average
\langle g\rangle
of
g
on
\mathcal{C}
are defined as follows: for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}
,

\llbracket g\rrbracket(\sigma):=\lim_{r\to 0}\big[g(\sigma+rn_{x}(\sigma))-g(\sigma-rn_{x}(\sigma))\big]\text{ and }\langle g\rangle(\sigma):=\dfrac{1}{2}\lim_{r\to 0}\big[g(\sigma+rn_{x}(\sigma))+g(\sigma-rn_{x}(\sigma))\big].

Above
n_{x}
denotes the outward normal vector of
\mathcal{C}
.

(4)

Consider a time-dependent interface
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(t)
, with local parametrizations
\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}(t,s)
,
j\in\llbracket 1,J\rrbracket
defined on
I\times V_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. We assume that
\gamma_{j}\in\mathscr{C}(I,\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}(V_{j}))
, and for all
t\in I
,
\mathcal{C}(t)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular manifold of dimension
d-1
, that forms the boundary of an open, bounded and simply connected domain
D(t)
. We introduce the following space:

L^{p}(I,\;\;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):=\left\{g=g(t,x)\colon\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{I}\lVert g(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}dt<\infty\quad\text{ if }\quad 1\leqslant p<\infty,\\
\displaystyle\sup_{t\in I}\text{ess}\lVert g(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty\quad\text{ if }\quad p=\infty\end{cases}\right\}.

For
I=(0,T)
, the corresponding space will sometimes be shortly denoted by
L^{p}_{T}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

1.1.2.
Reformulation of the system

A suitable way to rewrite the system (
1.1
) is to introduce the so-called fractional density or concentration
c
:

c:=\dfrac{\rho_{+}}{\rho}.

From the first two equations of (
1.1
), one checks easily, assuming some regularity, that the concentration solves
the transport equation

(1.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c+u\cdot\nabla c=0.

Let us notice that the domain occupied by the phase
\pm
can be defined by:

D_{\pm}(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\rho_{\pm}(t,x)>0\}.

As well, one can write the domain occupied by the one phase fluid with the help of the concentration as follows:

(1.7)

\displaystyle D_{+}(t)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon c(t,x)=1\},\;\quad D_{-}(t)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon c(t,x)=0\}

and consequently the interface is:

\mathcal{C}(t):=\partial D_{+}(t)=\partial D_{-}(t).

Assume that the velocity is regular, for instance Lipschitz, then it admits a continuous flow map
X(t)
that helps together with (
1.6
) and (
1.7
) to obtain that the initial interface is just transported by the fluid

\mathcal{C}(t)=X(t)\mathcal{C}(0).

On the other hand, summing up the mass equation on the density of the one phase
\rho_{\pm}

\eqref{epNS}_{1}
, one obtains that the total density solves a mass equation
with velocity
u
. Besides, the density
\rho_{\pm}
of the one phase fluid can be written in terms of the total density and the concentration as follows:

\rho_{+}=c\rho\quad\text{ and }\quad\rho_{-}=(1-c)\rho,

thus, the pressure can be written as function of the total density and the concentration,
P=P(\rho,c)
, such that

P_{+}(\rho)=P(\rho,1)
and
P_{-}(\rho)=P(\rho,0)
as well as the viscosity. Hence, formally
(c,\rho,u)
solves:

(1.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c+u\cdot\nabla c=0,\\
\partial_{t}\rho+\dvg(\rho u)=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\dvg(\rho u\otimes u)+\nabla P(\rho,c)=\dvg(2\mu(\rho,c)\mathbb{D}u)+\nabla(\lambda(\rho,c)\dvg u).\end{cases}

Conversely, if
(c,\rho,u)
solves (
1.8
), one can set

\rho_{+}=c\rho\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{-}=(1-c)\rho

and checks easily that
\rho_{\pm}
solve the equations
\eqref{epNS}_{1}
. Just by replacing

\rho=\rho_{+}+\rho_{-}\quad\text{and}\quad c=\dfrac{\rho_{+}}{\rho_{+}+\rho_{-}}

in the pressure and the viscosity, one infers that
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-},u)
solves (
1.1
). In fact, we obtain that

(\rho_{+},\rho_{-},u)
solves (
1.1
) if and only if
(c,\rho,u)
solves (
1.8
) owing to some change of pressure and viscosity law, at least for classical solutions. The equivalence also holds true for weak solutions
à la Lions
(we mean weak solutions which satisfy the classical energy inequality associated with (
1.1
), see
[
34
]
) due to
the result in
[
34
, Lemma 2.5]
, which is part of the DiPerna-Lions theory. Therefore, we will only work with the system (
1.8
).

The equations (
1.8
) are supplemented with initial data

(1.9)

\displaystyle c_{|t=0}=c_{0},\quad\;{\rho}_{|t=0}=\rho_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{|t=0}=u_{0}.

Given the equilibrium state of the pressure
\widetilde{P}
introduced in (
1.2
), the equilibrium state of the density,
\widetilde{\rho}
, is defined by the following relation:

(1.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}=P(\widetilde{\rho},1).

Next, we define the equilibrium states of the viscosities:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}:=\mu(\widetilde{\rho},1)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\lambda}:=\lambda(\widetilde{\rho},1).

We recall that
\mu
,
\lambda
and
P
are
\mathscr{C}^{2}
-regular, with
P
and
\lambda
being non-negative, while
\mu(\rho,c)>0
for
\rho>0
.

The purpose of this paper is to construct a unique local-in-time solution
(c,\rho,u)
of the equations (
1.8
) with discontinuous initial data and with density-dependent viscosity: the initial density
\rho_{0}
is assumed to be Hölder continuous on both sides of a suitable interface

\mathcal{C}_{0}
through which it is discontinuous. The initial velocity belongs to the Sobolev space
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

with the following compatibility condition:

(1.11)

\displaystyle\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This condition expresses the continuity of the normal component of the stress tensor and does not require smoothness of the density.
The parabolic effect of the momentum equations ensures that (
1.11
) holds true at positive times even for less regular initial data (see Hoff
[
13
]
). In particular it does not prevent to extend solutions until blow-up.

Our result is more general than the one by Tani
[
32
]
(see
section

1.2
below for more details), as it allows for less regular initial data, interface, and it can serve as building blocks for global solutions.

We do not have enough information in order to prove existence of global weak solution for the two-fluid model, even in the case of small initial data. However, we are able to prove
a global well-posedness result in the spirit of
[
14
]
for a simplified situation which is the Navier-Stokes equations with density-dependent viscosity. To keep the paper a reasonable size, we plan to address this issue in the near future.

1.2.
Review of known results

The study of the two-phase model (
1.1
) goes back to Tani’s work
[
32
]
where the author showed existence of a local-in-time solution for the heat conductive variant of (
1.1
). As in the pioneering work of Nash
[
26
]
, Tani’s proof relies on a change of variables into Lagrangian coordinates, followed by the construction of an approximate solution using the kernel associated to the linearized equations.
The solution is constructed in piecewise Hölder spaces : the density is
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
and the velocity is
\mathscr{C}^{2+\alpha}
,
on both sides of an interface
\mathcal{C}
which is
\mathscr{C}^{2+\alpha}
. To the best of our knowledge, Tani’s result is the only one addressing the two-fluid model with Hölder regularity for interfaces.

In the literature, other results mostly focus on interfaces with Sobolev regularity. As an example, Denisova in
[
8
]
has proved the local well-posedness of the system (
1.1
) including surface tension with different constant viscosity for each component of the mixture and with Sobolev-Slobodetskii interface
\mathcal{C}\in W^{\frac{5}{2}+l,2}
,
with
l\in(1/2,1)
. Her proof also relies on a change in Lagrangian coordinates and the density and the velocity are piecewise
W^{1+l,2}
regular on both sides of the interface
\mathcal{C}
.

In contrast to the aforementioned results based on a change in Lagrangian coordinates, there are other works
[
17
,
19
,
18
]
by Jang
et al.
that rely on a change into flattened coordinates.
The authors considered the two-phase problem in a cylinder, taking into account scenarios with and without surface tension. This problem involves two fluids, with one fluid on top of another. In this context, there are two interfaces to consider: the interface between the lower and upper fluids, and the interface between the upper fluid and the atmosphere. Both interfaces are assumed to be represented by graphs. Additionally, the viscosity of the two fluids are assumed to be constant and different from each other. In
[
17
]
, they successfully constructed local-in-time solution in Sobolev
framework, in
[
18
]
, they studied the Rayleigh-Taylor instability which occurs when the heavier fluid is on top of a lighter fluid. They successfully proved that when the surface tension is below a critical value, then the system is
nonlinearly unstable. Finally in
[
19
]
, they proved that if the heavier fluid is on top of the lighter one and the surface tension is sufficiently large, then, the Rayleigh-Taylor instability is prevented and in the converse
configuration, the system is stable around the equilibrium state with non-negative surface tension. Moreover, they
studied the zero surface tension limit problem.

On the other hand, there are also results
[
31
,
36
]
that address the two-phase problem involving a compressible fluid and an incompressible fluid.
In
[
31
]
, Shibata has proved the local well-posedness of this two-phase model, including temperature and surface tension, with an almost flat interface. The author worked on an exterior domain and with a viscosity that depends on both density and temperature. The initial density of the compressible fluid has a Sobolev
W^{1,p}
regularity and the initial velocity and temperature have the Besov regularity
B^{2(1-1/p)}_{q,p}
, on both sides of the interface, for some
1<p,q<\infty
that verify:

\dfrac{2}{p}+\dfrac{d}{q}<1.

The same Compressible-Incompressible model is considered in
[
36
]
by Yan and Zhao with surface tension in horizontal periodic domain. The viscosity of the fluids involved are different from each other and assumed to be constant.
They successfully achieved a global well-posedness result by making the assumption that the initial density of the compressible fluid is close to a constant state in
H^{4}
with respect to the domain occupied by the compressible fluid, and the initial velocity is small in
H^{4}
on both sides of the interface. To our knowledge, this result stands as the only one to address the existence of strong global solution of the two-phase problem.

Finally, we may notice that Kubo
et al.
in
[
22
]
have addressed the local well-posedness of the Compressible-Compressible model with different approach. Their methods rely on proving the existence of a
\mathcal{R}
-bounded solution operator and maximal regularity of the linearised system. The interface is uniformly
W^{r,2-1/r}
for
r>d
, the density has a
W^{1,r}
regularity on both sides of the interface and the initial velocity belongs to the Besov space
B^{2(1-1/p)}_{r,p}
for some
p>2
on both sides of the initial interface. More recently, in
[
23
]
, Kuo and Shibata have constructed local-in-time solution of the one phase model in half-space with non-slip boundary conditions. As the result above, the solution is also formulated, in some maximal regularity Besov space framework.

As explained, for instance in
[
12
]
, a different approach to tracking the interface is to construct weak
solutions for the full model in a class that allows the study of its dynamic. To the best of our knowledge, the only
available results for weak solutions pertain to fluid mixture with constant viscosity that are the same for both components.
For instance, we refer to the work by Vasseur, Wen, and Yu
[
34
]
in a three-dimensional bounded domain where they obtained a weak solution with finite initial energy. The main difficulty in their proof lies in the compactness of an approximate sequence of the pressure that depends on two variables, in contrast to the single-variable case
[
24
,
9
]
. They successfully overcome the difficulty by considering the following pressure law

P(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})=A_{+}{\rho_{+}}^{\gamma_{+}}+A_{-}{\rho_{-}}^{\gamma_{-}}

with the following closeness constraints on
\gamma_{\pm}
:

\gamma_{\pm}>9/5\quad\text{and}\quad\max\left\{\dfrac{3\gamma_{+}}{4};\gamma_{+}-1;\dfrac{3(\gamma_{+}+1)}{5}\right\}<\gamma_{-}<\min\left\{\dfrac{4\gamma_{+}}{3};\gamma_{+}+1;\dfrac{5\gamma_{+}}{3}-1\right\}.

This result has been improved by Novotný
[
27
]
, Wen
[
35
]
by allowing more general pressure law, Novotný and Pokortný
[
28
]
and Novotný
et al.

[
21
]
for multi-components fluid models. It is important to notice that, although the pressure law of the components of the mixture are different, the viscosity of the components are supposed to be the same constant. The solutions that the authors constructed are too weak in order to track down discontinuities in the density. In one dimension, Bresch, Burtea and Lagoutière in
[
2
]
successfully solved the global well-posedness for non common constant viscosity, with numerical considerations in
[
3
]
.

A class of weak solutions with more regularity (for the one phase model) has been proposed in the pioneering work of Hoff
[
13
]
, see also
[
29
,
33
]
for bulk viscosity coefficient
\lambda
depending on the density and Perepelitsa
[
30
]
for the case of bounded domains. However, these papers do not address the density patch problem.
This issue is explored in Hoff
[
14
]
and Hoff and Santos
[
15
]
, which examine the dynamics of sharp interfaces for the single-phase model, namely the Navier-Stokes equations with constant viscosity.
Persistence of
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular interface is achieved in
[
14
]
for
d=2
, under the condition that the density is piecewise
\alpha
-Hölder continuous. However,
the analysis is restricted to a linear pressure law and small viscosity
\lambda
. In
[
15
]
the density is only bounded, the gradient of the velocity is only
BMO
in space and for interfaces that are initially
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
one can only ensure
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha(t)}
-regularity for
t>0
where
\alpha(t)
is decaying exponentially to zero. Although not stated, a similar result i.e. propagation of Hölder regularity with exponential loss also holds in the context of
[
29
,
33
]
in which the viscosity
\lambda
is allowed to depend on the density, the analysis being very similar to
[
15
]
. However, when the viscosity
\mu
depends on the density, there is no clear notion of effective flux, the analysis complicates and it is not even clear how can one propagate the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the density, we refer to an interesting work by Bresch and Jabin
[
4
]
for a discussion regarding the complications arising in this case.

In this paper, we consider density-dependent viscosity for both components of the mixture, and we achieve the construction of unique weak solution in
\mathbb{R}^{d},\,(d\in\{2,3\})
for the system (
1.8
) which allows the study of the dynamics of the interface.

1.3.
Main result

We consider the Cauchy problem associated with equations (
1.8
) and initial data (
1.9
). We assume the
existence of an open, bounded, and simply connected domain
D_{0}
such that:

(1.12)

\displaystyle c_{0}=\mathbb{1}_{D_{0}^{c}}.

Also, we assume that the boundary
\mathcal{C}_{0}:=\partial D_{0}
fulfills the conditions outlined in
section

1.1.1

item

1
.
Additionally, the initial density
\rho_{0}
and velocity
u_{0}
satisfy:

(1.13)

\displaystyle u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad P(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{P}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\text{and}\quad\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

A technical step requires us to obtain estimate for the
L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
norm of
\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}\nabla\dot{u}
, where

\sigma(t)=\min\{1,t\}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{u}:=\partial_{t}u+(u\cdot\nabla)u

is the material acceleration of fluid particles. To achieve this, we need to look at the second material derivative of the velocity, which is

\ddot{u}:=\partial_{t}\dot{u}+(u\cdot\nabla)\dot{u}.

This variable appears while applying the material derivative
\partial_{t}\,\cdot+\dvg(\;\cdot\;u)
to the momentum equation
\eqref{ep1.1}_{3}
.
Our main result reads as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(c_{0},\rho_{0},u_{0})
be initial data associated with the system (
1.8
), satisfying the conditions (
1.10
), (
1.12
) and (
1.13
). Additionally, assume:

(1.14)

\displaystyle\rho_{*,0}:=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{0}(x)>0\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu_{*,0}:=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu(\rho_{0}(x),c_{0}(x))>0.

There exists
[\mu]>0
depending only on the dimension
d\in\{2,3\}
,
\alpha
and
\widetilde{\mu}
such that if
1
1

1

We refer to (
1.4
)-(
1.5
) for the definition of
\ell_{\varphi_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}
.

(1.15)

\left[1+\lVert\lambda(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\varphi_{0}}\right)\left\lVert\llbracket\lambda(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}\right]\lVert\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{\mu}\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\varphi_{0}}\right)\left[\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}+\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket,\,\llbracket\lambda(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}\left\|1-\dfrac{\widetilde{\mu}}{\langle\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rangle}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}\right]\leqslant\dfrac{[\mu]}{4},

then, there exist a time
T>0
and a unique solution
(c,\rho,u)
of the system (
1.8
) verifying the following:

(1)

\forall\;0<t\leqslant T,\;\;c(t)=\mathbb{1}_{D(t)^{c}}
with
\displaystyle\partial D(t)=:\mathcal{C}(t)
and with local parametrization
\gamma(t)\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
;

(2)

P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
;

(3)

u\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}((0,T),\dot{W}^{1,6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{16}((0,T),\dot{W}^{1,8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\sigma^{r/4}\nabla u\in L^{4}((0,T),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for

(1.16)

\displaystyle r=\begin{cases}\max\left(1/3,\,2\alpha\right)\quad&\text{if}\quad d=2,\\
\max\left(\dfrac{9}{14},\,2\alpha\right)\quad&\text{if}\quad d=3,\;\alpha\in(0,1/2),\\
\dfrac{2}{3}(5\alpha-1)\quad&\text{if }\quad d=3,\;\alpha\in[1/2,1).\end{cases}

(4)

\dot{u}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla\dot{u}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}\nabla\dot{u}\in L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
;

(5)

\sqrt{\sigma}\ddot{u}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\sigma\ddot{u}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

To prove
1.1
, we transform the system (
1.8
) into Lagrangian coordinates, see (
2.7
) below, to fix the domain. The existence of a unique solution
(\overline{c},\overline{\rho},\overline{u})
of (
2.7
) is obtained in
2.2
below. The flow of
\overline{u}
is regular enough to ensure that, back in Eulerian coordinates,
(c,\rho,u)
possesses all the properties stated in
1.1
.

Remark 1.2
.

(1)

The smallness assumption (
1.15
) arises from the study of the linearized equations associated with the equations resulting from the transformation of (
1.8
) into Lagrangian coordinates:

\rho_{0}v_{t}-\dvg(\mu\mathbb{D}v+\lambda\dvg vI_{d})=\dvg F.

To obtain
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and piecewise Hölder estimates for
\nabla u
, we express (see (
3.21
)):

\displaystyle\nabla v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\mu}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{P}\dvg F-\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg F\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\widetilde{\mu}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{P}\left(\rho_{0}v_{t}\right)+\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho_{0}v_{t})\right]

(1.17)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\widetilde{\mu}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{P}\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}v\}-\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}v\}\right].

where
\mathcal{P}
is the Leray projector onto the space of divergence-free vector fields. Under the smallness condition (
1.15
),
the last two terms of (
1.17
) are small in comparison to the left-hand side.

(2)

One of the main difficulties is to establish an estimate for the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-norm for
\nabla v
, (see page
3.2.1
). Except for the third term in the expression (
1.17
), which is regular in space, all other terms are second-order or fourth-order Riesz transforms of discontinuous functions (see the regularity of
\mu
,
\lambda
and
F
in (
3.2
)-(
3.3
)). It is well known that Riesz operators are not continuous on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which makes
the estimation of the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-norm of
\nabla u
more challenging. We employ a quantitative piecewise Hölder estimate for even-order Riesz operators obtained by Gancedo and García-Juárez (see
A.1
or
[
12
,
11
]
) to derive a piecewise Hölder estimate for
\nabla v
. Roughly speaking, the authors proved that these operators are continuous on
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
q\in[1,\infty)
, with a norm that may depend on the regularity of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
(see
section

1.1.1
-item 1).

Remark 1.3
.

Compared to Tani’s work
[
32
]
, we only require the interface
\mathcal{C}
to be
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular
and the density to be piecewise
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-Hölder continuous. Furthermore, initially non-Lipschitz velocities are allowed.
Indeed, for constant viscosity coefficients, the compatibility condition (
1.11
) reads:

\widetilde{\mu}\Delta u_{0}+(\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\lambda})\nabla\dvg u_{0}=\nabla P(\rho_{0})+\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0},

which leads to (similar to the derivation of (
1.17
)):

\displaystyle\nabla u_{0}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{\mu}}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathcal{P}\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0}-\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\lambda}}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\big(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}\big)\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0}

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\lambda}}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(P(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{P}).

We observe that, in general, the first two terms are not bounded for
\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 1.4
.

The solution
(c,\rho,u)
constructed in the above theorem exhibits the following properties.

(1)

There exists a constant
R
depending on the lifespan
T
, on the norms of the initial data and
on the initial interface
\mathcal{C}_{0}
such that:

\sup_{[0,T]}\left\{\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert\dot{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\}\leqslant R.

(2)

From
item

4
, we have
\rho\dot{u}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

and from the momentum equation (
1.8
) we infer that
\dvg(\Pi)\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
:
the compatibility condition (
1.13
) is propagated in time.

(3)

From
item

3
and interpolation we infer that the velocity
u
is continuous in time and space. Additionally, from
item

4
, its material derivative,
\dot{u}
is continuous in space. Furthermore, from
item

5
we deduce that
\ddot{u}
is at least continuous across the interface

\mathcal{C}(t)
. Roughly, for all
j\in\llbracket 1,J\rrbracket
,
s\in V_{j}
, we have:

\llbracket\dot{u}(t,\gamma_{j}(t,s))\rrbracket=0\implies\dfrac{d}{dt}\llbracket\dot{u}(t,\gamma_{j}(t,s))\rrbracket=\llbracket\ddot{u}(t,\gamma_{j}(t,s)\rrbracket=0.

(4)

From
item

3
, we infer that
\nabla u\in L^{1}((0,T),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and hence we have the persistence of the initial interface’s regularity. Indeed
\mathcal{C}(t)
is
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular manifold of dimension
d-1
, with the following estimates:

(1.18)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{C}(t)\right\rvert_{*}&\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lvert\mathcal{C}_{0}\right\rvert_{*}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u(\tau)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d\tau\right),\\
\displaystyle\lVert\nabla\gamma_{j}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}&\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\lVert\nabla\gamma_{0,j}\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\lVert\nabla\gamma_{0,j}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{1+\alpha}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u(\tau)\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}(\tau)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d\tau\right)\\
&\times\exp\left((2+\alpha)\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u(\tau)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d\tau\right).\end{cases}

(5)

Applying
\partial_{t}\cdot\,+\dvg(\,\cdot\;u)
to the momentum equations
\eqref{ep1.1}_{3}
, we find:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\rho\ddot{u}=\dvg(2\mu(\rho,c)\mathbb{D}\dot{u})+\nabla(\lambda(\rho,c)\dvg\dot{u})+\dvg\mathcal{F},

where
\mathcal{F}
is a lower order term that belongs, at least, to
L^{2}((\tau,T),\;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, for all
\tau\in(0,T)
. With the help of
item

5
, small fluctuation assumption on
\mu(\rho,c)
, quantitative Hölder estimate
A.1
, we infer from (
1.19
) that:

\nabla\dot{u}\in L^{2}((\tau,T),\;\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\quad\text{with}\quad\beta=\begin{cases}\alpha,\quad\text{in}\quad d=2,\\
\min\{\alpha,\,1/2\},\quad\text{in}\quad d=3,\end{cases}

for all
\tau\in(0,T)
.
In particular,
\nabla\dot{u}
is also continuous on both sides of
\mathcal{C}
.

1.1
immediately implies:

Corollary 1.5

(Blow-up criterion)
.

Let
(c,\rho,u)
be the solution constructed in
1.1
defined up to a maximal time
T^{*}
. If
T^{*}<\infty
, then we have either:

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to T^{*}}

\displaystyle\left\{\left\lvert\mathcal{C}(t)\right\rvert_{*}+\lVert\mathcal{C}(t)\rVert_{\text{Lip}}+\lVert\mathcal{C}(t)\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}+\bigg\|\dfrac{1}{\rho(t)},\;\;\dfrac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)(t)}\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\}

\displaystyle+\limsup_{t\to T^{*}}

\displaystyle\left\{\lVert u(t)\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert(\rho\dot{u})(t)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert P(\rho,c)(t)-\widetilde{P}\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\}=\infty,

or

(1.20)

\limsup_{t\to T^{*}}\left[1+\lVert\lambda(\rho,c)(t)\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}(t)}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\varphi(t)}\right)\left\lVert\llbracket\lambda(\rho,c)(t)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}(t))}\right]\lVert\mu(\rho,c)(t)-\widetilde{\mu}\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
+\limsup_{t\to T^{*}}\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}(t)}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\varphi(t)}\right)\left[\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)(t)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}(t))}+\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)(t)\rrbracket,\llbracket\lambda(\rho,c)(t)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}(t))}\left\|1-\dfrac{\widetilde{\mu}}{\langle\mu(\rho,c)(t)\rangle}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}(t))}\right]\geqslant[\mu].

Remark 1.6
.

Our result can be seen as a first step towards the existence of global solutions with small data which we plan to attack in a forthcoming paper. The construction of discontinuous weak-solutions for compressible models with variable viscosity cannot be achieved in the classical way by regularizing the initial data and applying some classical results
à la Matsmura-Nishida
since this procedure
destroys
the discontinuities in the density. Some difficult constructions are then needed in order to obtain discontinuous weak-solutions, see
[
14
, Section 3]
. We believe that since we dispose of powerful functional analysis tools, see
appendix

A
, an approach where local-well posedness is first proved to be quite natural. With
1.1
in hand, in order to attack the global existence issue, we do not have to worry anymore about constructing appropriate approximate systems that preserve the main feature of the system (
1.8
).

Remark 1.7
.

The difficulty in proving global existence for the two-fluid model lies in the fact that we are not able to estimate the
L^{1}((0,T),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

norm of
\nabla u
uniformly with respect to time. In particular, see (
1.18
), the characteristic
\left\lvert\mathcal{C}(t)\right\rvert_{*}
and the
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-norm of the interface
\gamma_{j}(t)
are expected to grow exponentially with respect to time which prevents the validity of the condition (
1.20
) globally in time.

In the study of the Navier-Stokes equations
with constant viscosity for
d=2
, Hoff
[
14
]
has observed that the exponential-in-time growth of these quantities can be compensated by the exponential decay of the pressure jump across the interface (in our case we would be needing the exponential-in-time decay of the viscosity jump, see the explosion criterion (
1.20
)). In order to obtain exponential decay, we use the continuity of the normal component of the stress tensor, we follow the arguments starting from (
3.39
) below to establish:

\llbracket(2\mu(\rho,c)+\lambda(\rho,c))\dvg u-P(\rho,c)\rrbracket=2\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\left(\langle\dvg u\rangle-\langle\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\rangle n^{j}_{x}n^{k}_{x}\right).

Next, we observe that
f=f(\rho,c)
defined up to a constant as

f(\rho,c)=\int^{\rho}\dfrac{2\mu(s,c)+\lambda(s,c)}{s}ds

satisfies:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(\rho,c)+u\cdot\nabla f(\rho,c)+P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}=-\left[(2\mu(\rho,c)+\lambda(\rho,c))\dvg u-P(\rho,c)\right].

We write the above equation along the flow
X
of the velocity and then take jumps to obtain:

(1.21)

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dt}\llbracket f(\rho,c)\rrbracket(t,X(t,s))+\llbracket P(\rho,c)\rrbracket(t,X(t,s))=2\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\left(\langle\dvg u\rangle-\langle\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\rangle n^{j}_{x}n^{k}_{x}\right)(t,X(t,s)),\;s\in\mathcal{C}_{0}.

Observe that in the constant viscosity case, the RHS of the above equation vanishes and using the fact that the pressure is strictly increasing one infers in a straightforward manner the exponential-in-time decay of the jump of
f
.

For the two-fluid model, when the pressure and viscosity laws of the two phases are different, it is less obvious how to obtain exponential decay of the jump or even if this property holds in general
. First of all the RHS of the above identity is no longer
0
and second of all in order to obtain exponential-in-time decay, the jump of
f,P
and
\mu
need to be "comparable", (this is the case for constant
\mu
and increasing
P=P(\rho)
). For multi-phase flows the minimal requirements seem to be that:

(1.22)

\displaystyle 0<\text{\textnu}_{*}\leqslant\dfrac{P(\rho,1)-P_{-}(\rho^{\prime},0)}{f(\rho,1)-f(\rho^{\prime},0)}\leqslant\text{\textnu}^{*}\quad\text{ and }\quad\dfrac{\lvert\mu(\rho,1)-\mu(\rho^{\prime},0)\rvert}{\lvert f(\rho,1)-f(\rho^{\prime},0)\rvert}+\dfrac{\lvert\lambda(\rho,1)-\lambda(\rho^{\prime},0)\rvert}{\lvert f(\rho,1)-f(\rho^{\prime},0)\rvert}\leqslant\text{\textnu}^{*},

at least, for all
\rho
close to
\widetilde{\rho}(1)
and
\rho^{\prime}
close to
\widetilde{\rho}(0)
where
\widetilde{P}=P(\widetilde{\rho}(1),1)=P(\widetilde{\rho}(0),0)
. This requirement does not seem to occur in general. For instance, it does not hold in the case of two different constant viscosity coefficients say
\mu(\rho,1)=\widetilde{\mu},\;\mu(\rho,0)=\mu_{-}
, where
\mu_{-}\neq\widetilde{\mu}
.

Outline of the paper

The remainder of this paper is divided into three sections. In the first one,
section

2
, we rewrite system (
1.8
) in Lagrangian coordinates, see (
2.7
) below, and in the second one,
section

3
, we study the linearized system associated to (
2.7
). Finally, the study of the full nonlinear system in Lagrangian coordinates (
2.7
) is presented in
section

4
from which
1.1
follows.

2.
Change of variables in Lagrangian coordinates

The aim of this section is to write the system (
1.8
)
in Lagrangian coordinates. We know that for a Lipschitz vector field
u
, one can define
a flow map
\mathscr{X}
given by the Duhamel formula:

\mathscr{X}(t,y)=y+\int_{0}^{t}u(s,\mathscr{X}(s,y))ds

and adopting the following notation: for all function
g=g(t,x)
we denote by
\overline{g}
the function:

\overline{g}(t,y):=g(t,\mathscr{X}(t,y))

then, the flow of the vector field
u
can be written in term of
\overline{u}
by :

\mathscr{X}(t,y)=:\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)=y+\int_{0}^{t}\overline{u}(s,y)ds.

And for any
g=g(t,x)
, one has that

(2.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{g}(t,y)=(\partial_{t}g+u\cdot\nabla g)(t,\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)).

We introduce some notations.

Notation 2.1
.

Let us fix a square matrix
B=(b_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

•

The trace of
B
is the sum of the diagonal elements of
B
:

\text{Tr}(B)=b_{ii}.

The transposed matrix of
B
, denoted
B^{T}
, is:

B^{T}=(b_{ji})_{ij}

and for
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

\text{Tr}(B\cdot B^{\prime})=b_{ij}b^{\prime}_{ji}=B\colon B^{\prime}=(B^{\prime})^{T}\colon B^{T}.

•

For
i,j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
,
M_{ij}(B)
denotes the determinant of the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix
obtained by removing the
i
-th row and
j
-th column of
B
.

•

The adjugate matrix of
B
, denoted as
\adj(B)
, is given by:

(\adj(B))_{ij}=(-1)^{i+j}M_{ji}(B).

•

If
\det(B)\neq 0
, then
B
is invertible and we have:

B^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{\det(B)}\adj(B).

If
DG
denotes the Jacobian matrix of
G
, then chain rule yields:

D\overline{G}=DG\circ\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}\cdot D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}},

but, since

(2.2)

\displaystyle(D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y))_{ij}=\delta^{ij}+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{j}\overline{u}^{i}(s,y)ds,

it holds, at least for small time, that the Jacobian matrix of the change of variable is invertible and hence:

\overline{DG}(t,y)=D\overline{G}(t,y)\cdot(D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y))^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)}D\overline{G}(t,y)\cdot\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y).

In particular, one has that:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial_{j}G^{i}}=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}}\partial_{k}\overline{G}^{i}\left(\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}\right)_{kj}.

On the other hand, from Piola’s formula: for any matrix
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{R})
,

\partial_{k}(\adj(A))_{kj}=0

we infer that:

(2.4)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial_{j}G^{i}}=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}}\partial_{k}\left[\overline{G}^{i}\left(\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}\right)_{kj}\right].

The first form (
2.3
) will be referred as the non-conservative form and the second (
2.4
) is the conservative one and both will
be used. From the non-conservative form, we find

\overline{\dvg G}=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}}\text{Tr}(D\overline{G}\cdot\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}),

and by setting

A_{\overline{u}}(t,y)=(D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y))^{-1}

we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\dvg_{A_{\overline{u}}}G=\overline{\dvg G}=D\overline{G}:A_{\overline{u}}=A^{T}_{\overline{u}}:\nabla\overline{G}.

On the other hand, for all matrix
B\in\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T],\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R}))
, one has:

\displaystyle\dfrac{d\det B}{ds}(s)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial\det B}{\partial b_{ij}}\dfrac{\partial b_{ij}}{\partial s}(s)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+j}M_{ij}(B(s))\dfrac{\partial b_{ij}}{\partial s}(s)

\displaystyle=\adj(B(s))_{ji}\left(\dfrac{\partial B}{\partial s}(s)\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial B(s)}{\partial s}:\adj(B(s)).

In particular, for
B=D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}
, and remembering from (
2.2
) that

\dfrac{\partial D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}}{\partial t}=D\overline{u}

we find

\dfrac{\partial\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}}{\partial t}(t,y)=D\overline{u}(t,y):\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)).

Hence, the identity (
2.5
) yields:

(2.6)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}}{\partial t}(t,y)=\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)\dvg_{A_{\overline{u}}}u(t,y).

Finally, combining (
2.1
) and the previous identity, we notice that the continuity equation on the density
reads:

\dfrac{\partial(\overline{\rho}\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}})}{\partial t}(t,y)=0

and consequently

\overline{\rho}(t,y)=\dfrac{\rho_{0}(y)}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)}.

Now, let us proceed to rewrite the momentum equations in Lagrangian coordinates. First, from what precedes, it is clear that

\left[\partial_{t}(\rho u^{j})+\dvg(\rho u^{j}u)\right](t,\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y))=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)}\rho_{0}(y)\partial_{t}\overline{u}(t,y)

so it only remains to compute the term involving the stress tensor. From the conservative form (
2.4
),
we have:

\displaystyle\overline{\dvg\Pi^{j}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)}\partial_{k}\left\{(\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}})_{kl}\overline{\Pi}^{j}_{l}\right\}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}(t,y)}\partial_{k}\left\{(\adj D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}})_{kl}\left\{2\mu(\overline{\rho},c_{0})\mathbb{D}_{A_{\overline{u}}}^{jl}\overline{u}+\left(\lambda(\overline{\rho},c_{0})\dvg_{A_{\overline{u}}}\overline{u}-P(\overline{\rho},c_{0})+\widetilde{P}\right)\delta_{jl}\right\}\right\}

where, we use the following notation:

2\mathbb{D}_{B}v=Dv\cdot B+B^{T}\cdot\nabla v.

This ends the transformation of the momentum equation in Lagrangian coordinates.
Summing up, we find that
(\overline{c},\overline{\rho},\overline{u})
solves:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\overline{c}=0,\\
\partial_{t}\left\{\overline{\rho}J_{\overline{u}}\right\}=0,\\
\rho_{0}\partial_{t}\overline{u}=\dvg\left\{\adj(D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}})\left\{2\mu(\overline{\rho},c_{0})\mathbb{D}_{A_{\overline{u}}}\overline{u}+\left(\lambda(\overline{\rho},c_{0})\dvg_{A_{\overline{u}}}\overline{u}-P(\overline{\rho},c_{0})+\widetilde{P}\right)I_{d}\right\}\right\},\end{cases}

where
J_{\overline{u}}:=\lvert\det D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}}\rvert
. We establish the well-posedness of (
2.7
) in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume the hypotheses in
1.1
hold true for
(c_{0},\rho_{0},u_{0})
, the pressure law, and the viscosity laws
\mu
,
\lambda
. Additionally, suppose that the small fluctuation assumption (
1.15
) also holds true, and consider
r
as given in (
1.16
).

Then, there exist
T>0
and a unique solution
\overline{u}
of the Cauchy problem

(2.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho_{0}\partial_{t}\overline{u}=\dvg\left\{\adj(D\mathscr{X}_{\overline{u}})\left\{2\mu(\rho_{0}J_{\overline{u}}^{-1},c_{0})\mathbb{D}_{A_{\overline{u}}}\overline{u}+\left(\lambda(\rho_{0}J_{\overline{u}}^{-1},c_{0})\dvg_{A_{\overline{u}}}\overline{u}-P(\rho_{0}J_{\overline{u}}^{-1},c_{0})+\widetilde{P}\right)I_{d}\right\}\right\},\\
\overline{u}_{|t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}

verifying the following:

(1)

\overline{u}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}((0,T),\dot{W}^{1,6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{16}((0,T),\dot{W}^{1,8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\sigma^{r/4}\nabla\overline{u}\in L^{4}((0,T),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
;

(2)

\partial_{t}\overline{u}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla\partial_{t}\overline{u}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}\nabla\partial_{t}\overline{u}\in L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
;

(3)

\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{tt}\overline{u}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\sigma\partial_{tt}\overline{u}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

In
section

3
below, we study the linearized equations associated with (
2.8
). The proof of the existence of
\overline{u}
is the purpose of
section

4
, and it relies on a fixed point argument. The proof of uniqueness follows a similar argument to that in
[
6
]
and is therefore omitted here. It only requires viscosity far from vacuum and the velocity gradient to meet the following conditions:

\nabla\overline{u}\in L^{1}((0,T),L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla\overline{u}\in L^{2}((0,T),L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

In particular, no small fluctuation assumption on the viscosity
\mu
is required. Once we have constructed
\overline{u}
,
we express
(\overline{c},\overline{\rho})
from
\eqref{ep2.7}_{1,2}
, and we finally go back into Eulerian coordinates to obtain that
(c,\rho,u)
solves (
1.8
) with initial data
(c_{0},\rho_{0},u_{0})
.
We now proceed to the study of the linearized equations associated with (
2.8
).

3.
Study of the linearised system

3.1.
Statement of the main result

The aim of this section is to establish the well-posedness of the linear non-homogeneous parabolic equation

(3.1)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}u_{t}-\dvg(2\mu\mathbb{D}u+\lambda\dvg uI_{d})=\dvg F

associated to (
2.7
). The initial density
\rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(x)
and the viscosity
\mu=\mu(t,x),\;\lambda=\lambda(t,x)
, are supposed to verify:

(3.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0<\rho_{*}\leqslant\rho_{0}\leqslant\rho^{*},\;0<\mu_{*}\leqslant\mu\leqslant\mu^{*},\;\;0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant\mu^{*};\\
\mu,\;\lambda\in L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}));\\
\mu_{t},\;\lambda_{t}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{1}((0,T),L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}));\\
\sqrt{\sigma}(\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t})\in L^{2}((0,T),L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\;\sigma(\mu_{tt},\lambda_{tt})\in L^{2}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\end{cases}

for positive constants
\rho_{*},\rho^{*},\mu_{*},\mu^{*}
and time
0<T<\infty
.
The source term
F
in (
3.1
) and the initial datum
u_{0}
are such that
(F,u_{0})\in Y_{T}(\mu_{0},\lambda_{0})
where:

\displaystyle Y_{T}(\mu_{0},\lambda_{0}):=\left\{(F,u_{0})\right.

\displaystyle\left.\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\colon F\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\right.

\displaystyle\left.F\in L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\;\sigma^{r/4}F\in L^{4}((0,T),\,\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\right.

\displaystyle\left.F_{t}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\;\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}F_{t}\in L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\right.

(3.3)

\displaystyle\left.\sigma F_{tt}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\;\dvg(2\mu_{0}\mathbb{D}u_{0}+\lambda_{0}\dvg u_{0}I_{d}+F_{|t=0})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}.

Above,
\mu_{0}:=\mu_{|t=0}
,
\lambda_{0}:=\lambda_{|t=0}
and
0<r<8/3
depends on
0<\alpha<1
and the dimension, see (
3.47
) below. The existence and uniqueness of solution to (
3.1
) is established in the following space:

\displaystyle X_{T}:=

\displaystyle\left\{u\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\colon\right.\nabla u\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\,\sigma^{r/4}\nabla u\in L^{4}((0,T),\,\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),

\displaystyle\left.u_{t}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\;\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla u_{t}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\;\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}\nabla u_{t}\in L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\right.

(3.4)

\displaystyle\left.\sqrt{\sigma}u_{tt}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\;\sigma u_{tt}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\right\}.

The time weights are inspired by the pioneering work of Hoff
[
13
]
. One of the main difficulties is to obtain estimates for
\nabla u
, the solution of (
3.1
), that allow us to propagate the regularity of the interface
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. The difficulty arises from the fact that the density and viscosity do not have any (global-in-space) Sobolev regularity, and therefore we are unable to differentiate (
3.1
) with respect to space.

The main result of this section is stated in the following theorem:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
, the viscosity
\mu,\,\lambda
and the initial density
\rho_{0}
verify (
3.2
),
0<\alpha<1
. There exists a constant
[\mu]>0
depending only on the dimension
d
,
\alpha
and
\widetilde{\mu}
such that if:

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\left[1+\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\varphi_{0}}\right)\lVert\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}\right]\sup_{[0,T]}\lVert\mu-\widetilde{\mu}\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(3.5)

\displaystyle+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\varphi_{0}}\right)\sup_{[0,T]}\left[\lVert\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}+\lVert\llbracket\mu\rrbracket,\,\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}\left\|\dfrac{\langle\mu-\widetilde{\mu}\rangle}{\langle\mu\rangle}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}\right]\leqslant[\mu],

then, for all
(F,u_{0})\in Y_{T}(\mu_{0},\lambda_{0})
, there exists a unique solution of the Cauchy problem:

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho_{0}u_{t}-\dvg(\mu\mathbb{D}u)-\nabla(\lambda\dvg u)=\dvg F,\\
u_{|t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}

in
X_{T}
. Moreover, the following estimates hold true with a constant
C_{*}
that depends polynomially on the upper and lower bounds of the density and viscosity (see first line of (
3.2
)):

•

Estimates for
u
and
u_{t}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\lVert u,\,u_{t},\,\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\nabla u,\,\nabla u_{t},\,u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant C_{*}\left(\lVert(F,u_{0})\rVert_{Y_{T}(\mu_{0},\lambda_{0})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}\cap L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right)

(3.7)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]\right).

•

Higher integrability estimates for
\nabla u
:

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vskip 2.84544pt\displaystyle\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+C_{*}\sup_{[0,T]}\lVert F,u_{t},\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\\
\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\left(\lVert F\rVert_{L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\lVert F,u_{t},\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right).\end{cases}

•

Estimates for
u_{tt}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\sqrt{\sigma}u_{tt},\;\sigma\nabla u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lVert\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla u_{t},\;\sigma u_{tt}\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\left[\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F_{t},\,\sigma F_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t},\,\sigma(\mu_{tt},\,\lambda_{tt})\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]

(3.9)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sigma\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]\right).

The norms of
u
involved in this estimate are controlled thanks to (
3.7
)-(
3.8
).

•

L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
norm estimate for
\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}\nabla u_{t}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}+C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}

(3.10)

\displaystyle+C_{*}\lVert\sigma u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\nabla u_{t},\,F_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\dvg u,\,\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\tfrac{8}{d}-2}.

•

Hölder estimate for
\nabla u
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{r}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{4}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{**}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{r}\lVert F\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{4}+C_{**}\lVert F,\,\nabla u,\,u_{t},\,\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{4}

(3.11)

\displaystyle+C_{**}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F,\,\nabla u,\,u_{t},\,\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]^{2}+C_{**}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}\right]^{d/2},

with a constant
C_{**}
that depends polynomially on the same parameters as
C_{*}
, and on
\alpha
,
\ell_{\varphi_{0}}^{-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}
and
\lVert\mu,\;\lambda\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{pw,\mathcal{C}_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

Remark 3.2
.

The first estimate (
3.7
) follows from the classical energy estimate and the so-called Hoff estimates
introduced in
[
13
,
14
]
. These estimates provide
H^{1}
regularity for the time derivative of the velocity
u
. The other estimate (
3.9
) can be seen as a higher-order Hoff estimate
and has be used in
[
7
]
in the study of the incompressible Navier-Stokes model.

The proof of
3.1
is the subject of
section

3.2
below and it is based on the classical method of a priori estimates combined with a homotopy argument.

3.2.
Proof of
3.1

The proof of
3.1
relies on a priori estimates and a homotopy argument and is structured into three sections: in
section

3.2.1
, we derive a priori estimates (
3.7
), (
3.8
), (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), whereas
section

3.2.2
-
section

3.2.3
focus on the homotopy argument.

3.2.1.
A priori estimates

The aim of this section is to derive the a priori estimates (
3.7
), (
3.8
), (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3.11
).
We observe that the regularity of
u
is sufficient to use use
u
or
u_{t}
as test functions in subsequent calculations. We use

C
to denote any constant that depends only on the dimension,
\widetilde{\mu}
, and
\alpha
. Conversely,
C_{*}
denotes a constant that may additionally depend polynomially on the lower and upper bounds of
\rho_{0}
,
\mu
and
\lambda
.

Proof of (
3.7
)
.

Here we derive energy estimates for
u
and
u_{t}
.
We take the scalar product of the equation (
3.6
) with the velocity
u
. By doing so, we obtain:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{d}{dt}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2\mu\lvert\mathbb{D}u\rvert^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda\lvert\dvg u\rvert^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}\partial_{k}u^{j}.

We then integrate the above in time and we have:

(3.12)

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u(t)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2\mu\lvert\mathbb{D}u\rvert^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda\lvert\dvg u\rvert^{2}=\dfrac{1}{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}\partial_{k}u^{j}.

Using the identity:

(3.13)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbb{D}w\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla w\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\dfrac{1}{2}\lVert\dvg w\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

we deduce that

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mu_{*}\left(\lVert\nabla w\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\lVert\dvg w\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right)\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2\mu(x)\lvert\mathbb{D}w(x)\rvert^{2}dx.

This inequality will be freely used in the forthcoming computations to translate the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-norm estimate of
\sqrt{\mu}\mathbb{D}w
into that of
\nabla w
with
w\in\{u,u_{t},u_{tt}\}
. For instance, by applying (
3.14
) with
w=u
and
using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain from (
3.12
):

(3.15)

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

Next, we use
u_{t}
as a test function in the weak formulation of (
3.1
) in order to obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu(t)\lvert\mathbb{D}u(t)\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda(t)(\dvg u(t))^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{0}\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{0}\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{0}(\dvg u_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}(s)\partial_{k}u^{j}(s)\bigg|_{s=0}^{s=t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}_{t}\partial_{k}u^{j}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}\lvert\mathbb{D}u\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}(\dvg u)^{2}.

In the following lines, we will estimate the right hand side in order to use Grönwall’s lemma. The third and the fourth terms are:

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}(s)\partial_{k}u^{j}(s)\bigg|_{s=0}^{s=t}\Bigg|\leqslant\eta\lVert\nabla u(t)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\dfrac{1}{4\eta}\lVert F(t)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\lVert\nabla u(0)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\lVert F(0)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},

for some positive
\eta>0
small in order to absorb the norm of the gradient in the left hand side of the above equality. Next, the fifth term can be bounded as follows:

\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}_{t}\partial_{k}u^{j}\bigg|\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

Finally, the last two terms are controlled by:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}\lvert\mathbb{D}u\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}(\dvg u)^{2}\leqslant C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\lVert\mu_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

Gathering all of these estimates, and choosing
\eta
small, one has:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\sup_{[0,t]}\lVert\nabla u\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\nabla u_{0}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C_{*}\sup_{[0,t]}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

(3.16)

\displaystyle+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

We differentiate (
3.6
) in time and we use
u_{t}
as a test function in the weak formulation of the resulting equation. By doing so, we obtain:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{0}\lvert u_{t}(t)\rvert^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2\mu\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{t}\rvert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda(\dvg u_{t})^{2}=\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{0}\lvert{u_{t}}_{|t=0}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\partial_{k}u^{j}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\dvg u\dvg u_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}_{t}\partial_{k}u^{j}_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{0}\lvert{u_{t}}_{|t=0}\rvert^{2}+\eta\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

(3.17)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},

with some positive
\eta
small related to the lower bound of the viscosity
\mu
. We now turn to the estimate of the
L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
norm of
\nabla u
that appears in the above inequality. We rewrite (
3.6
) as:

(3.18)

\displaystyle-\widetilde{\mu}\Delta u-\nabla\{(\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda)\dvg u\}=\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u\}-\rho_{0}u_{t}+\dvg F.

We denote by
\mathcal{P}=I-\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg
the usual projectors onto the spaces of divergence-free and curl-free vector fields, respectively.
First, we apply
\mathcal{P}
to (
3.18
) and we have:

(3.19)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}\mathcal{P}u=(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathcal{P}\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u+F\}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathcal{P}(\rho_{0}u_{t}).

Second, we apply the divergence operator to obtain:

(3.20)

\displaystyle(2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda)\dvg u=(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u+F\}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho_{0}u_{t})

and given that
\mathcal{Q}w=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg w
, we find:

\mathcal{Q}u=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u+F\}\right]+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho_{0}u_{t})\right]

Summing up, we express:

\displaystyle\nabla u

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathcal{P}u+\nabla\mathcal{Q}u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\mu}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\left[\mathcal{P}\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u+F\}-\mathcal{P}\left(\rho_{0}u_{t}\right)\right]+\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho_{0}u_{t})\right]

(3.21)

\displaystyle-\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u+F\}\right].

Furthermore, from (
3.18
), we have:

(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})=\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(2\mu\mathbb{D}u)+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\lambda\dvg u)+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg F

and owing to the fact that
\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla
is continuous over
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, we have:

(3.22)

\displaystyle\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\nabla u,F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Additionally, singular integral theory helps us to obtain from (
3.21
):

(3.23)

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leqslant\dfrac{C}{\widetilde{\mu}}\lVert\mu-\widetilde{\mu}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+C\lVert F\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+C\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Therefore, under the hypothesis (
3.5
), we absorb the first term
of the right hand side in the left one. With the help of Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality and (
3.22
), the last terms above can be bounded as (recall
d\in\{2,3\})
:

\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\rho_{0}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{d/4}\lVert\nabla u,F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-d/4}.

In consequence, the last term of (
3.17
) can be estimated as:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}

(3.24)

\displaystyle+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\lVert F,\;\nabla u,\;u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}.

We now use (
3.24
) in (
3.17
), choose
\eta
related to the lower bound of the viscosity and density in order to absorb the second term of the right hand side of (
3.17
) in the left hand side. Applying Gronwall’s Lemma and adding (
3.16
) we find:

(3.25)

\sup_{[0,T]}\lVert u_{t},\,\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\nabla u_{t},\,u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant C_{*}\left(\lVert(F,u_{0})\rVert_{Y_{T}(\mu_{0},\lambda_{0})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}\cap L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\right)\\
\times\exp\left(C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]\right).

Finally, (
3.7
) follows from combining (
3.25
) and (
3.15
). We now turn to the proof of (
3.8
).
∎

Proof of (
3.8
)
.

Here we derive
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-norm estimate for the velocity gradient.
According to (
3.21
), for
q\in\{6,8\}
we have:

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant\dfrac{C}{\widetilde{\mu}}\lVert\mu-\widetilde{\mu}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Therefore, under the hypothesis (
3.5
), we absorb the first term
of the right hand side in the left one. We only have to estimate the last term. Using Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality and (
3.22
) we obtain:

\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\rho_{0}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\beta}\lVert\nabla u,F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-\beta}

with
\beta=d/2-d/6\in\{2/3,1\}
. In consequence, we have:

(3.26)

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+C_{*}\sup_{[0,T]}\lVert F,u_{t},\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

For
q=8
and
d=2
, we have:

\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\rho_{0}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{3/4}\lVert\nabla u,F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1/4}

whereas for
d=3
, Sobolev’s and interpolation inequalities yield:

\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{P}(\rho_{0}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\rho_{0}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{24/11}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/8}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{7/8},

such that,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{16}((0,T),L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+C_{*}\lVert F,u_{t},\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+C_{*}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This concludes (
3.8
) and we turn to the proof of (
3.9
).
∎

Proof of (
3.9
)
.

Here we establish estimates for
u_{tt}
. We first differentiate equation (
3.6
) in time:

(3.28)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}u_{tt}-\dvg(2\mu\mathbb{D}u_{t}+\lambda\dvg u_{t}I_{d})=\dvg F_{t}+\dvg(2\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u+\lambda_{t}\dvg uI_{d}).

Testing (
3.28
) with
\sigma u_{tt}
(recall
\sigma(t)=\min\{1,t\}
and hence
\sigma(0)=0
) leads to:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sigma(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{t}\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{\sigma(t)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda\lvert\dvg u_{t}\rvert^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{t}\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda\lvert\dvg u_{t}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{t}\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\lvert\dvg u_{t}\rvert^{2}-2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}

(3.29)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\dvg u\dvg u_{tt}-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{t}^{jk}\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}.

Using Hölder’s inequality, the last term can be estimated as:

\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{t}^{jk}\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}\bigg|\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\dfrac{1}{4\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

and the remaining terms are bounded by:

\displaystyle\bigg|\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\lvert\dvg u_{t}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\dvg u\dvg u_{tt}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{t}\rvert^{2}-2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Gathering all of these estimates, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sigma(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu\lvert\mathbb{D}u_{t}\rvert^{2}+\dfrac{\sigma(t)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda\lvert\dvg u_{t}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{\prime}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+2\eta\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

(3.30)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\lVert\mu_{t},\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

The second term on the right hand side above will be reduced with the terms coming from the second energy which we turn to.
We differentiate (
3.28
) with respect to time before multiplying the resulting equation with
\sigma^{2}u_{tt}
. By doing so, we obtain:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\mu}\mathbb{D}u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\lambda}\dvg u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle-4\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u_{t}\partial_{k}u_{tt}^{j}-2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\dvg u_{t}\dvg u_{tt}-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}_{tt}\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}

\displaystyle-2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{tt}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{tt}\dvg u\dvg u_{tt}.

We estimate the second and third terms of the right hand side as follows:

\bigg|4\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u_{t}\partial_{k}u_{tt}^{j}+2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{t}\dvg u_{t}\dvg u_{tt}\bigg|\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{tt}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},

whereas the last two terms are bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg|2\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{tt}\mathbb{D}^{jk}u\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{tt}\dvg u\dvg u_{tt}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{tt}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\dfrac{C_{*}}{\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\mu_{tt},\,\lambda_{tt}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

Finally, the fourth term of the right hand side is:

\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{jk}_{tt}\partial_{k}u^{j}_{tt}\bigg|\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{tt}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\dfrac{1}{4\eta}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{2}\lVert F_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

Gathering all of these estimates, adding (
3.30
) and choosing
\eta
small related to the lower bounds of the viscosity, we finally apply Grönwall’s Lemma, leading to (noticing
\sigma^{\prime}(t)=0
for
t>1
):

(3.31)

\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\sigma u_{tt},\;\sigma^{2}\nabla u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+\lVert\sigma\nabla u_{t},\;\sigma^{2}u_{tt}\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leqslant C_{*}\left[\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F_{t},\,\sigma^{2}F_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right.\\
\left.+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t},\,\sigma\mu_{tt},\,\sigma\lambda_{tt}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]\exp\left(C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\right]\right).

This concludes (
3.9
) and we turn to (
3.10
).
∎

Proof of (
3.10
)
.

Here we derive
L^{8/d}((0,T),L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
-norm estimate for
\sigma^{\tfrac{d}{4}}\nabla u_{t}
.
Differentiating (
3.19
)-(
3.20
) with respect to time yields:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vskip 5.69046pt\widetilde{\mu}\mathcal{P}u_{t}&=(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathcal{P}\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u_{t}+2\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u+F_{t}\}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathcal{P}(\rho_{0}u_{tt}),\\
(2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda)\dvg u_{t}&=-\lambda_{t}\dvg u+(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u_{t}+2\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u+F_{t}\}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho_{0}u_{tt})\end{cases}

and therefore (recall
\mathcal{Q}w=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg w
):

\displaystyle\nabla u_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathcal{P}u_{t}+\nabla\mathcal{Q}u_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\mu}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\left[\mathcal{P}\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u_{t}+2\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u+F_{t}\}-\mathcal{P}\left(\rho_{0}u_{tt}\right)\right]+\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho_{0}u_{tt})\right]

\displaystyle+\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{\lambda_{t}}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}\dvg u\right]-\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\dfrac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\{2(\mu-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u_{t}+2\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u+F_{t}\}\right].

Whence, under the hypothesis (
3.5
) we obtain:

\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C_{*}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C_{*}\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{tt})\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and we only need to estimate the last term. Following the computations leading to (
3.22
), we have from (
3.28
):

(3.32)

\displaystyle\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{tt})\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\nabla u_{t},\,F_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\dvg u,\,\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

and Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality implies:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla(\rho_{0}u_{tt})\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{t},\,F_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\dvg u,\,\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\tfrac{8}{d}-2}\lVert u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\lVert\sigma u_{tt}\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\nabla u_{t},\,F_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\dvg u,\,\mu_{t}\mathbb{D}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\tfrac{8}{d}-2}.

In summary, we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert F_{t}\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}+C_{*}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}\lVert\mu_{t},\,\lambda_{t}\rVert_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{8/d}
